---
**Overview**
Xi Jinping was the dominant ruler of China in 2023. He held the formal posts of general secretary of the Chinese Communist Party, president of the PRC, and chairman of both the party and state Central Military Commissions, giving him direct control over party, state, and military channels. In practice, his authority was constrained less by independent institutions than by internal party incentives, bureaucratic implementation problems, elite turnover, local-government debt and fiscal stress, and external pressure from the U.S. and regional rivals. The 2023 period mixed high centralization, continued repression, a fragile post-COVID recovery, and escalating external friction, especially around Taiwan and the South China Sea.

**Evidence Records**
1. **Formal authority and real power**
- **Fact:** Xi held the core state and party offices in 2023, including CPC general secretary, PRC president, and chair of both CMCs; this made him the top decision-maker across party, state, and military systems.
- **Why it matters:** This is the baseline for attributing policy outcomes, repression, diplomacy, and military decisions to Xi.
- **Strongest source:** *Xi Jinping unanimously elected Chinese president, PRC CMC chairman*, Xinhua via National People’s Congress, March 10, 2023. https://en.npc.gov.cn.cdurl.cn/2023-03/11/c_868263.htm
- **Support locator:** Search result snippet states he was unanimously elected president and CMC chairman; official bio also lists his concurrent offices.
- **Period:** Current to 2023.
- **Connection to ruler:** Direct and maximal.
- **Complicating evidence:** Formal titles do not prove unilateral control over every outcome; implementation still depended on party organs and the state bureaucracy.
- **Caution:** Official PRC reporting is polished and omits institutional friction.
- **Independent support:** Xinhua bio page; CPC constitution; foreign-policy analyses noting “one-man politics.”
- **Later retrospective evidence:** None needed.

2. **Institutional limits inside the PRC system**
- **Fact:** China’s constitution says the NPC is the “highest state organ of power,” but it is controlled by a party-dominated political system in which the NPC, state council, courts, and military are structurally subordinate rather than independent.
- **Why it matters:** It clarifies the main limit on Xi’s power: not competitive institutions, but internal party hierarchy, bureaucratic execution, and policy tradeoffs.
- **Strongest source:** *Constitution of the People’s Republic of China*, NPC. https://www.npc.gov.cn/englishnpc/constitution2019/201911/t20191120_384295.html
- **Support locator:** Articles 57, 58, 62, 67.
- **Period:** Inherited structure, still operative in 2023.
- **Connection to ruler:** Indirect but important: Xi operates inside this system and dominates it through party office.
- **Complicating evidence:** The constitution formally gives the NPC and its Standing Committee powers that are broader than their real political autonomy.
- **Caution:** Constitutional text describes formal powers, not actual contestation.
- **Independent support:** CFR’s 2023 analysis that the NPC and advisory bodies “hold much power” and are ritualized around Xi; Reuters reporting on direct leadership in elite appointments.
- **Later retrospective evidence:** None needed.

3. **Chinese modernization as Xi’s main domestic program**
- **Fact:** In 2023 Xi repeatedly framed the regime’s main goal as “Chinese modernization,” tied to deeper reform, opening up, self-reliance in core technologies, and rural revitalization.
- **Why it matters:** This is the clearest statement of the ruler’s agenda and the standard against which 2023 performance should be judged.
- **Strongest source:** *Xi stresses deepening reform, expanding opening up, advancing Chinese modernization*, Xinhua via State Council portal, April 13, 2023. https://english.www.gov.cn/news/202304/13/content_WS6437e054c6d03ffcca6ec3dc.html
- **Support locator:** The article says Xi urged Guangdong to lead in advancing Chinese modernization, stressed self-reliance in key technologies, and linked reform to modernization.
- **Period:** 2023.
- **Connection to ruler:** Direct.
- **Complicating evidence:** Later 2023 official and outside reports suggest the economy remained fragile and reform momentum was uneven.
- **Caution:** Official framing emphasizes goals, not failures or tradeoffs.
- **Independent support:** Xinhua/CAC transcript of the April 21 reform commission meeting.
- **Later retrospective evidence:** None needed.

4. **Post-COVID social control and protest suppression**
- **Fact:** In 2023 China kept tight control over memory and dissent, including heavy policing around Tiananmen commemoration and detention of people in Hong Kong around the June 4 anniversary.
- **Why it matters:** It shows how the state managed protest risk, public memory, and civil liberties under Xi.
- **Strongest source:** *China tightens access to Tiananmen Square, 32 detained in Hong Kong on anniversary of 1989 protests*, Associated Press, June 4, 2023. https://www.kpbs.org/news/international/2023/06/04/china-tightens-access-to-tiananmen-square-while-32-are-detained-in-hong-kong
- **Support locator:** Article text says discussion and commemorations are forbidden within China and that people were detained in Hong Kong around Victoria Park.
- **Period:** 2023.
- **Connection to ruler:** Strong, through party-state security policy and Hong Kong governance under national security law.
- **Complicating evidence:** Authorities justify these measures as maintaining public order and national security.
- **Caution:** AP relies on field reporting and police accounts; exact detention totals varied across versions.
- **Independent support:** Reuters images of detentions in Hong Kong; U.S. State Department and HRW reporting on broader repression.
- **Later retrospective evidence:** None needed.

5. **Opaque elite removal and centralization**
- **Fact:** Qin Gang’s sudden removal in July 2023, after disappearing from public view, showed how opaque elite politics remained under Xi and how much key appointments depended on direct top-level control.
- **Why it matters:** It is a concrete example of centralized rule, weak transparency, and elite instability in a strategic ministry.
- **Strongest source:** Reuters via Investing, *“Not found”: China’s ex-foreign minister is gone but wait for explanation goes on*, July 31/August 1, 2023. https://www.investing.com/news/world-news/analysisnot-found-chinas-exforeign-minister-is-gone-but-wait-for-explanation-goes-on-3139809
- **Support locator:** Lines 420-429 note Qin’s removal, his disappearance from ministry pages, the health explanation, and the lack of a public reason.
- **Period:** 2023.
- **Connection to ruler:** Strong but indirect; reflects elite management under Xi rather than a single policy choice.
- **Complicating evidence:** The foreign ministry later offered minimal explanation; the underlying cause remained unconfirmed.
- **Caution:** Reuters inferred political significance from opaque official behavior.
- **Independent support:** Multiple Reuters analyses; official ministry silence itself.
- **Later retrospective evidence:** None needed.

6. **Military purge and corruption inside the PLA**
- **Fact:** By late 2023 Xi’s military modernization drive was complicated by a sweeping purge of generals, especially around the Rocket Force and military procurement circles.
- **Why it matters:** This directly affects war risk, command confidence, and the credibility of Xi’s military build-up.
- **Strongest source:** Reuters via Investing, *Sweeping Chinese military purge exposes weakness, could widen*, December 30/31, 2023. https://www.investing.com/news/world-news/analysissweeping-chinese-military-purge-exposes-weakness-could-widen-3264891
- **Support locator:** The story says the purge weakened the PLA, exposed corruption, and could slow modernization.
- **Period:** 2023, with immediate end-of-year developments.
- **Connection to ruler:** Strong; the anti-corruption campaign and appointments sit under Xi’s CMC authority.
- **Complicating evidence:** The purge may also have strengthened discipline and loyalty over the medium term.
- **Caution:** Reuters relies partly on analyst judgment because PRC details were sparse.
- **Independent support:** Washington Post analysis on the new defense minister; Axios summary; Reuters reporting on removed officials.
- **Later retrospective evidence:** None needed.

7. **External security tension, especially Taiwan and the South China Sea**
- **Fact:** 2023 saw continued coercive military signaling around Taiwan and repeated maritime confrontation with the Philippines in the South China Sea.
- **Why it matters:** These are the clearest external conflict risks attributable to Xi’s rule in this period.
- **Strongest source:** Reuters via Investing, *Philippines, China trade accusations over South China Sea collision*, December 9/10, 2023. https://www.investing.com/news/world-news/philippines-china-trade-accusations-over-south-china-sea-collision-3253180
- **Support locator:** Lines 425-427 describe the collision, water cannon use, and competing blame.
- **Period:** 2023.
- **Connection to ruler:** Strong on overall strategic direction, though the immediate incident was operational.
- **Complicating evidence:** Beijing framed the actions as defense of sovereignty; in January 2023 China and the Philippines also agreed to manage disputes peacefully.
- **Caution:** Maritime incidents are dispute-driven and each side gives a different account.
- **Independent support:** Reuters on April 2023 drills around Taiwan; Reuters timeline on 2023 South China Sea escalations.
- **Later retrospective evidence:** None needed.

8. **Economic recovery, but fragile and uneven**
- **Fact:** China’s 2023 recovery was real but fragile: growth rebounded, yet the property sector, weak confidence, debt, and weak external demand kept the outlook soft.
- **Why it matters:** This is the main domestic performance question for Xi in 2023.
- **Strongest source:** World Bank press release and December 2023 China Economic Update. https://www.worldbank.org/en/news/press-release/2023/12/14/sustained-policy-support-and-deeper-structural-reforms-to-revive-china-s-growth-momentum-world-bank-report
- **Support locator:** Lines 154-160 say activity picked up, but the recovery remained fragile; lines 156-158 project 5.2% growth in 2023 with risks from real estate, debt, and weak demand.
- **Period:** 2023.
- **Connection to ruler:** Strong, because macroeconomic strategy is under central political control, though not all outcomes are directly attributable to Xi alone.
- **Complicating evidence:** Official policy support did stabilize parts of the economy, and some sectors improved.
- **Caution:** World Bank language is cautious and macro-level; it does not isolate Xi’s personal contribution.
- **Independent support:** Reuters on weak property sales and stimulus calls; World Bank December 2023 report.
- **Later retrospective evidence:** None needed.

9. **Welfare and public-services stress**
- **Fact:** Even after poverty reduction gains, international reviewers still found major disparities in access to housing, health care, education, sanitation, and safe water, especially for migrants and rural residents.
- **Why it matters:** This tempers any claim that economic recovery translated into broad living-standard gains.
- **Strongest source:** UN Committee on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights concluding observations on China, March 3, 2023. https://docstore.ohchr.org/SelfServices/FilesHandler.ashx?enc=O9c0oeLiDcuqy70zYHxSCBHtyCr6MB3teJ%2BWiG2MvjkskkO5QF1LREC8LJ2s%2BnCPOvTA3EAyrbja%2FTqdkfU1Vw%3D%3D
- **Support locator:** Lines 219-220 on persistent regional and urban-rural disparities; lines 227-228 on inadequate health-care funding; lines 245-248 on education access.
- **Period:** Mostly inherited conditions still visible in 2023.
- **Connection to ruler:** Moderate; these are governance outcomes under Xi’s system, not a single decision.
- **Complicating evidence:** The committee also noted progress in poverty reduction and a larger health-care budget.
- **Caution:** UN committees are strongly rights-based and may emphasize shortcomings over incremental gains.
- **Independent support:** World Bank work on social safety nets and hukou reform; HRW on post-COVID social control.
- **Later retrospective evidence:** None needed.

**Information Environment**
- Reliable information was hard to obtain on many politically sensitive topics.
- The U.S. State Department’s 2023 China report described no independent judiciary, pervasive surveillance, restrictions on expression, internet censorship, arbitrary detention, and serious limits on elections and political participation. Source: https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107650.html
- HRW’s 2023 UPR submission said repression deepened under Xi’s third term and that authorities tightened control over civil society, media, and the internet. Source: https://www.hrw.org/news/2023/07/27/submission-universal-periodic-review-china
- The UN CESCR committee noted poor data-sharing, fragmented COVID implementation, and weak transparency around rights and social indicators. Source: https://docstore.ohchr.org/SelfServices/FilesHandler.ashx?enc=O9c0oeLiDcuqy70zYHxSCBHtyCr6MB3teJ%2BWiG2MvjkskkO5QF1LREC8LJ2s%2BnCPOvTA3EAyrbja%2FTqdkfU1Vw%3D%3D
- For military and elite politics, official details were sparse; Reuters-style reporting was often inferential because the government gave little explanation.
- The main caution is source asymmetry: PRC official sources are useful for formal positions and stated goals, but they omit failure, conflict, and coercion; outside human-rights sources are strong on repression but may be less complete on positive administrative outcomes.

**Questions for Deeper Research**
- How directly did Xi personally drive the Qin Gang removal and the 2023 PLA purge?
- What was the internal balance between party control and technocratic economic management during the property downturn?
- How much did 2023 coercive control in Hong Kong and mainland China change compared with 2022?
- Did the 2023 economic rebound materially improve household welfare, or was it concentrated in state-backed investment and industry?
- How much did the Rocket Force purge affect near-term operational readiness?
- Which 2023 policies were preventive, reactive, or purely rhetorical?

**Corroborating Sources**
- World Bank China Economic Update, December 2023: https://documents.worldbank.org/en/publication/documents-reports/documentdetail/099558301102431321
- Reuters on property weakness and stimulus: https://www.investing.com/news/economic-indicators/china-nov-industrial-output-up-66-yy-retail-sales-below-expectations-3257152
- Reuters on Taiwan drills: https://apnews.com/article/2023-china-taiwan-drills-reuters
- Reuters on July 2023 Qin Gang removal: https://www.investing.com/news/world-news/analysisnot-found-chinas-exforeign-minister-is-gone-but-wait-for-explanation-goes-on-3139809
- Reuters on South China Sea tensions: https://www.investing.com/news/world-news/china-philippines-trade-accusations-over-collision-in-south-china-sea-3204930

**Promising Leads**
- UN treaty-body materials on China’s 2023 review of civil, political, economic, and social rights.
- Reuters/AP coverage of the April 2023 Taiwan drills and the November 2023 APEC/Xi-Biden diplomacy.
- Official PRC speeches on Chinese modernization and reform commission meetings.
- High-quality reporting on the defense minister change and the Rocket Force purge.
- Source-discovery follow-up on 2023 public-health, education, and social-welfare execution data.

--- RESEARCH HANDOFF ---

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 1B ---

**Orientation**
Xi Jinping was China’s top political and military decision-maker in 2023, but the 1B lens is mostly about what he did with that authority on nuclear and other catastrophic-risk issues. The 2023 baseline was not a new nuclear doctrine but rapid force expansion, tighter control, and persistent opacity. The main safety-relevant shocks were continued PLA modernization, silo construction, the broader U.S.-China strategic competition, China’s posture on Ukraine and arms control, and the persistent lack of transparent operational data. Information quality was uneven: China made strong public no-first-use claims, but independent observers repeatedly noted that arsenal size, command arrangements, and readiness were opaque.

**Evidence Index**
1. **No-first-use and anti-nuclear-war rhetoric**
- Xi and Chinese official diplomacy continued to affirm that nuclear weapons must not be used and nuclear war must not be fought.
- Strongest current-period source: Xi-Macron joint statement, April 6, 2023.
- Supports lenses: `1B.2`, `1B.4`, `1B.8`.

2. **Risk-reduction diplomacy in the NPT/P5 setting**
- China participated in P5 nuclear-risk discussions and continued to present itself as a risk-reduction actor.
- Strongest sources: Arms Control Association coverage of the February 2023 P5 meeting in Dubai; China’s August 2, 2023 UN working paper on nuclear risk reduction.
- Supports lenses: `1B.1`, `1B.2`, `1B.4`, `1B.8`.

3. **Rapid nuclear buildup and silo expansion**
- China was materially expanding its nuclear forces, including multiple new silo fields and increased readiness.
- Strongest sources: FAS February 10, 2023 analysis; FAS 2024 Nuclear Notebook.
- Supports lenses: `1B.1`, `1B.3`, `1B.5`, `1B.10`.

4. **Uncertain command, control, and safety transparency**
- Public sources remained thin on custody, command, and safety arrangements; the best outside assessments emphasized uncertainty and secrecy.
- Strongest sources: FAS status page; FAS/ACA nuclear modernization analysis; UN and official sources by omission.
- Supports lenses: `1B.3`, `1B.7`, `1B.10`.

5. **Arms-control posture was selective, not disarmament-oriented**
- China backed risk-reduction language but did not join the Treaty on the Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons, and its official position remained firmly deterrence-based.
- Strongest sources: UN Treaty Collection; China’s official MFA statements.
- Supports lenses: `1B.4`, `1B.2`, `1B.5`.

6. **Potentially destabilizing use of nuclear capability as strategic cover**
- 2023 military signaling and the larger modernization program increased deterrent leverage, but also raised escalation and misperception risk.
- Strongest sources: FAS/ACA analyses; official PRC joint statements with France.
- Supports lenses: `1B.5`, `1B.8`, `1B.10`.

7. **Other catastrophic-risk domains**
- In the material inspected here, 2023 sources on AI/cyber/biological catastrophe were not as developed as the nuclear file; the strongest usable evidence remained nuclear and strategic-risk focused.
- Supports lenses: mainly `1B.9` as a gap, not a fulfilled record.

**Evidence Records**

1. **No-first-use rhetoric in 2023**
- **Claim:** Xi publicly reaffirmed that “nuclear weapons must not be used and nuclear wars must not be fought” in April 2023, while tying the statement to restraint in the Ukraine war and opposition to attacks on nuclear facilities.
- **Why it matters:** This is the clearest 2023 statement of China’s claimed nuclear-risk posture under Xi. It supports a responsible-rhetoric finding only if matched by doctrine and practice.
- **Best source:** *Xi Jinping: China Ready to Issue a Joint Call with France for Political Settlement of the Ukraine Crisis*, Ministry of Foreign Affairs of the PRC, April 6, 2023. https://www.fmprc.gov.cn/eng/wjb/zzjg_663340/xos_664404/xwlb_664406/202304/t20230406_11055500.html
- **Locator:** The joint press statement section listing nuclear weapons, nuclear war, and opposition to attacks on nuclear power plants.
- **Period fit:** Target-period.
- **Attribution basis:** Direct statement by Xi in an official foreign-ministry record.
- **Complicating evidence:** The same period also featured continued arsenal expansion and silo construction.
- **Cautions:** Official transcript; designed for diplomatic messaging.
- **Independent support:** China’s 2022 P5 joint statement; later 2024 MFA no-first-use initiative; Arms Control Association coverage of P5 risk-reduction diplomacy.
- **Lens mapping:** `1B.2`, `1B.8`, partly `1B.4`.

2. **China kept engaging in P5 nuclear-risk talks**
- **Claim:** In February 2023, the five NPT nuclear-weapon states met in Dubai to discuss reducing the risk of nuclear war, and China remained part of that forum.
- **Why it matters:** It shows China was still participating in the main multilateral risk-reduction channel for nuclear-weapon states.
- **Best source:** *NPT Nuclear-Weapon States Meet in Dubai*, Arms Control Association, March 2023. https://www.armscontrol.org/act/2023-03/news-briefs/npt-nuclear-weapon-states-meet-dubai
- **Locator:** Lines 79-82.
- **Period fit:** Target-period.
- **Attribution basis:** China’s presence in the P5 meeting and risk-reduction agenda.
- **Complicating evidence:** Participation in discussion is not the same as verifiable policy restraint.
- **Cautions:** Secondary source; brief coverage rather than full transcript.
- **Independent support:** China’s own 2022 joint statement with the other four nuclear-weapon states.
- **Lens mapping:** `1B.1`, `1B.2`, `1B.4`, `1B.8`.

3. **China’s UN nuclear-risk-reduction working paper**
- **Claim:** China submitted a UN working paper titled *Nuclear Risk Reduction* on August 2, 2023.
- **Why it matters:** This is the strongest primary evidence that China formally presented itself as supporting risk reduction in the NPT process during 2023.
- **Best source:** *Nuclear Risk Reduction : working paper / submitted by China*, UN Digital Library, August 2, 2023. https://digitallibrary.un.org/record/4017563
- **Locator:** Record metadata: symbol `NPT/CONF.2026/PC.I/WP.30`, date, and title.
- **Period fit:** Target-period.
- **Attribution basis:** Direct Chinese submission.
- **Complicating evidence:** The record metadata alone does not show the full argument; separate analysis is needed to test substance.
- **Cautions:** PDF content was not retrievable in this session; record-level metadata only.
- **Independent support:** Arms Control Association reporting on NPT risk-reduction talks; China’s official no-first-use statements.
- **Lens mapping:** `1B.1`, `1B.4`, `1B.8`.

4. **China’s nuclear-submarine paper shows AUKUS-focused risk framing**
- **Claim:** China also submitted a separate 2023 UN working paper on nuclear submarine cooperation involving AUKUS, indicating that Beijing treated allied nuclear-submarine arrangements as a nonproliferation/risk issue.
- **Why it matters:** This is relevant to proliferation control and to how China framed strategic risk in 2023.
- **Best source:** *Nuclear Submarine Cooperation among the United Kingdom of Great Britain and Northern Ireland and the United States of America and Australia : working paper / submitted by China*, UN Digital Library, August 2, 2023. https://digitallibrary.un.org/record/4017564?ln=en
- **Locator:** Record metadata: symbol `NPT/CONF.2026/PC.I/WP.31`, date, and title.
- **Period fit:** Target-period.
- **Attribution basis:** Direct Chinese submission.
- **Complicating evidence:** This is criticism of others’ conduct, not proof of China’s own restraint.
- **Cautions:** Metadata only; PDF not accessible here.
- **Independent support:** China’s broader risk-reduction and anti-proliferation rhetoric; later follow-up submissions in 2024.
- **Lens mapping:** `1B.4`, `1B.6`, `1B.9` as indirect context.

5. **China did not join the TPNW**
- **Claim:** China remained outside the Treaty on the Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons in 2023.
- **Why it matters:** It shows China did not embrace the strongest formal disarmament treaty available to nuclear-armed states.
- **Best source:** UN Treaty Collection, TPNW status page. https://treaties.un.org/Pages/ViewDetails.aspx?chapter=26&clang=_en&mtdsg_no=XXVI-9&src=IND
- **Locator:** Status lines showing 95 signatories and 75 parties, with no Chinese signature or accession.
- **Period fit:** Target-period and current status.
- **Attribution basis:** Treaty-status record.
- **Complicating evidence:** China argues for no-first-use and gradual disarmament outside TPNW.
- **Cautions:** Treaties page is status-only; it does not explain motives.
- **Independent support:** China’s own official no-first-use and risk-reduction statements.
- **Lens mapping:** `1B.4`, `1B.2`.

6. **China’s nuclear modernization accelerated**
- **Claim:** By early 2023, China was already building several hundred new missile silos, with the strategic buildup viewed as rapid and unprecedented.
- **Why it matters:** This is the core adverse fact for existential-risk responsibility: even with responsible rhetoric, the actual force posture was expanding quickly.
- **Best source:** *STRATCOM Says China Has More ICBM Launchers Than The United States – We Have Questions*, Federation of American Scientists, February 10, 2023. https://fas.org/publication/stratcom-says-china-has-more-icbm-launchers-than-the-united-states/
- **Locator:** Lines 46-57.
- **Period fit:** Mostly inherited into 2023, but directly relevant in 2023.
- **Attribution basis:** Direct strategic modernization under Xi-era CMC control.
- **Complicating evidence:** Some launcher counts may include silos not yet operational, so exact operational status was uncertain.
- **Cautions:** Open-source counting is inferential.
- **Independent support:** FAS 2024 Chinese Nuclear Forces assessment.
- **Lens mapping:** `1B.1`, `1B.3`, `1B.5`, `1B.10`.

7. **China’s expanded silo fields and launch posture raised launch-on-warning concerns**
- **Claim:** By mid-2023 China had begun constructing three large silo fields of roughly 100 silos each, and analysts noted that the expansion could reflect interest in a launch-on-warning posture.
- **Why it matters:** This directly concerns command-and-control risk and crisis stability.
- **Best source:** *The Unknowns About China’s Nuclear Modernization Program*, Arms Control Association, June 2023. https://www.armscontrol.org/act/2023-06/features/unknowns-about-chinas-nuclear-modernization-program
- **Locator:** Lines 102-104 and 88-89 for readiness changes.
- **Period fit:** Target-period with inherited buildup.
- **Attribution basis:** PLA Rocket Force construction under Xi’s military authority.
- **Complicating evidence:** The article stresses uncertainty; silo construction does not by itself prove launch-on-warning adoption.
- **Cautions:** Secondary analysis; some conclusions are inferential.
- **Independent support:** FAS counts and Pentagon estimates referenced inside the article.
- **Lens mapping:** `1B.3`, `1B.8`, `1B.10`.

8. **China’s arsenal kept growing and public transparency stayed limited**
- **Claim:** FAS estimated in 2026 that China had about 620 warheads in its military stockpile and that the Chinese stockpile was increasing; it also said China had never publicly disclosed the size of its arsenal or much of the supporting infrastructure.
- **Why it matters:** Even though the estimate is retrospective, it helps bound the 2023 posture and shows the longstanding opacity that makes reliability difficult.
- **Best source:** *Status of World Nuclear Forces*, Federation of American Scientists, March 2025 page with updated estimates. https://fas.org/initiative/status-world-nuclear-forces/
- **Locator:** Lines 49-53, 60-64, 71, 97-98.
- **Period fit:** Later retrospective and contextual.
- **Attribution basis:** Open-source estimate of force posture under Xi-era expansion.
- **Complicating evidence:** The exact number is uncertain and updated over time.
- **Cautions:** This is an estimate, not a PRC disclosure.
- **Independent support:** 2024 FAS Chinese nuclear notebook.
- **Lens mapping:** `1B.3`, `1B.10`, context for `1B.1`.

9. **China’s 2024 Nuclear Notebook found a large and rapid expansion**
- **Claim:** FAS’s 2024 Nuclear Notebook said China’s nuclear expansion was among the largest and most rapid modernization campaigns of the nine nuclear-armed states, with continued silo-field development and additional delivery-system changes.
- **Why it matters:** This is the clearest concise open-source summary of the direction of travel coming out of 2023.
- **Best source:** *Chinese Nuclear Forces, 2024: A “Significant Expansion”*, FAS, January 16, 2024. https://fas.org/publication/chinese-nuclear-forces-2024-a-significant-expansion/
- **Locator:** Lines 47-57.
- **Period fit:** Later retrospective on 2023 posture.
- **Attribution basis:** Open-source defense assessment.
- **Complicating evidence:** The report acknowledges substantial uncertainty and dependence on assumptions.
- **Cautions:** Retrospective and estimate-heavy.
- **Independent support:** June 2023 Arms Control Association article; Pentagon projections discussed by FAS.
- **Lens mapping:** `1B.1`, `1B.3`, `1B.10`.

10. **China’s official rhetoric linked nuclear restraint to general strategic stability**
- **Claim:** China repeatedly argued that all nuclear-weapon states should reduce the role of nuclear weapons and avoid launch-on-warning and preemptive strategies.
- **Why it matters:** This shows the official normative framing Xi’s government used in 2023, even as its own arsenal expanded.
- **Best source:** PRC MFA statement on maintaining global strategic stability and reducing nuclear-conflict risk. https://www.fmprc.gov.cn/mfa_eng/gjhdq_665435/3376_665447/3432_664920/3435_664926/202406/t20240612_11428155.html
- **Locator:** Passage calling for no-first-use, abandoning launch-on-warning, and reducing nuclear roles.
- **Period fit:** Later publication, but reflects China’s continuing official line.
- **Attribution basis:** Official Chinese diplomatic position.
- **Complicating evidence:** Official rhetoric coexisted with silo construction and arsenal growth.
- **Cautions:** Later retrospective source.
- **Independent support:** April 2023 Xi-Macron statement; 2022 P5 leaders’ statement.
- **Lens mapping:** `1B.2`, `1B.4`, `1B.8`.

11. **China’s nuclear-power expansion is not the same as nuclear-weapon risk, but it shows a broader nuclear-industrial capability**
- **Claim:** By the end of 2023, China had 55 operational and 36 approved-or-under-construction civil nuclear power units, and authorities highlighted a self-reliant nuclear supply chain.
- **Why it matters:** This is not a weapons claim, but it is relevant background for national nuclear capacity and institutional competence.
- **Best source:** *China's nuclear power generation reaches 440,000 GWh in 2023*, Xinhua via State Council portal, April 23, 2024. https://english.www.gov.cn/archive/statistics/202404/23/content_WS6627b28fc6d0868f4e8e658e.html
- **Locator:** Article lines on 55 operational units, 36 approved or under construction, and the self-reliant supply chain.
- **Period fit:** Later retrospective.
- **Attribution basis:** Official Chinese nuclear-sector data.
- **Complicating evidence:** Civil nuclear capacity does not imply safer nuclear governance.
- **Cautions:** Not a weapons source; retrospective.
- **Independent support:** Separate 2023 nuclear-energy sector reporting.
- **Lens mapping:** Limited relevance to `1B.9` as background only.

12. **The broader arms-control environment remained divided**
- **Claim:** At the September 2023 NPT meeting, China was still grouped with states calling for resumed arms-control dialogue and multilateral engagement, but the overall process remained divided.
- **Why it matters:** It places China’s nuclear posture in a wider diplomatic context rather than treating its statements in isolation.
- **Best source:** *NPT Meeting Underscores Chronic Divisions*, Arms Control Association, September 2023. https://www.armscontrol.org/act/2023-09/news/npt-meeting-underscores-chronic-divisions
- **Locator:** Line 82.
- **Period fit:** Target-period.
- **Attribution basis:** Multilateral diplomatic record.
- **Complicating evidence:** The same meeting showed chronic failure to bridge major-power divisions.
- **Cautions:** Short analytical piece.
- **Independent support:** China’s own UN working paper and PRC statements.
- **Lens mapping:** `1B.4`, `1B.8`.

**Information Environment and Limitations**
- China’s own public messaging strongly emphasized no-first-use, strategic stability, and anti-nuclear-war language, but it offered little granular information on warhead numbers, C2 arrangements, custody, readiness, or silo operational status.
- Open-source nuclear analysts repeatedly had to infer from satellite imagery, procurement traces, and foreign government estimates.
- The evidence base is therefore asymmetric:
- Official PRC material is strongest on declared doctrine and diplomatic posture.
- Independent analysts are strongest on arsenal growth and operational uncertainty.
- The main caution is not just propaganda, but structural secrecy.

**Important Questions for Deeper Research**
- Did Xi personally endorse any shift toward launch-on-warning or only tolerate force-structure changes that could support it?
- How much of the silo buildout was operational by the end of 2023?
- Did China’s 2023 risk-reduction diplomacy have any effect on actual force posture, or was it largely rhetorical?
- Was the Rocket Force purge in late 2023 connected to safety, corruption, or loyalty enforcement?
- What concrete safeguards existed for nuclear command, custody, and deconfliction in 2023?

**Corroborating Sources**
- *Chinese Nuclear Forces, 2024: A “Significant Expansion”*, FAS. https://fas.org/publication/chinese-nuclear-forces-2024-a-significant-expansion/
- *Status of World Nuclear Forces*, FAS. https://fas.org/initiative/status-world-nuclear-forces/
- *The Unknowns About China’s Nuclear Modernization Program*, Arms Control Association. https://www.armscontrol.org/act/2023-06/features/unknowns-about-chinas-nuclear-modernization-program
- *NPT Nuclear-Weapon States Meet in Dubai*, Arms Control Association. https://www.armscontrol.org/act/2023-03/news-briefs/npt-nuclear-weapon-states-meet-dubai
- PRC MFA no-first-use and stability statements. https://www.fmprc.gov.cn/eng/wjb/zzjg_663340/xos_664404/xwlb_664406/202304/t20230406_11055500.html
- UN Treaty Collection TPNW status page. https://treaties.un.org/Pages/ViewDetails.aspx?chapter=26&clang=_en&mtdsg_no=XXVI-9&src=IND

**Promising Leads Still Needing Inspection**
- The full PDF text behind China’s August 2023 UN working paper on nuclear risk reduction.
- The full PDF text behind China’s 2023 AUKUS-related nuclear submarine working paper.
- The 2023 Pentagon China military report sections on nuclear force growth and command posture.
- Deeper open-source work on the Rocket Force purge and whether it touched safety or command procedures.
- Specialist work on AI, cyber, and biological catastrophic-risk controls in China during 2023.

**Source Counts**
- Opened and used as evidence: 12
- Opened and used mainly for corroboration/context: 4
- Promising leads not fully inspected in this pass: 4+
- Access-blocked or metadata-only: UN Digital Library PDF content and some SCIO pages

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 2B ---

**Orientation**
Xi Jinping was China’s central authority in 2023, but this chapter is mostly about how that authority was used in international security and peace policy rather than domestic repression. The 2023 baseline was a hardening strategic environment: Taiwan tensions, South China Sea coercion, the Ukraine war, U.S.-China rivalry, and a continuing Chinese preference for bilateral leverage over binding conflict-limitation structures. China’s official line combined peace language, selective diplomacy, and legalistic statements with force modernization and coercive signaling. Information quality was mixed: official PRC texts were strong for formal positions and diplomatic acts, while outside reporting and specialist analysis were needed to test what those positions meant in practice.

**Evidence Index**
1. **Diplomatic peace posture on Ukraine**
- Xi’s government publicly supported political settlement language and issued peace-oriented statements on Ukraine in 2023.
- Best evidence: PRC MFA readout of Xi’s April 6, 2023 call with Macron.

2. **Peace mediation in Saudi-Iran diplomacy**
- China helped broker the March 2023 Saudi-Iran normalization deal, a major diplomatic success for de-escalation.
- Best evidence: PRC official joint statement and contemporaneous reporting.

3. **Taiwan coercion**
- China continued large-scale military pressure around Taiwan in April 2023.
- Best evidence: Reuters/AP coverage of the drills.

4. **South China Sea escalation**
- China continued coercive maritime behavior against the Philippines in 2023.
- Best evidence: Reuters coverage of collisions and water-cannon incidents.

5. **Lawful-security rhetoric, but selective compliance**
- China used peace and stability rhetoric, but it remained outside some major arms-control commitments and continued to reject constraints on its own force posture.
- Best evidence: UN treaty status, PRC diplomatic texts, Arms Control Association analysis.

6. **No broad war initiation, but continued coercive security behavior**
- There was no China-initiated conventional interstate war in 2023, but there was sustained coercive pressure and militarized diplomacy.
- Best evidence: Taiwan drills, South China Sea incidents, diplomacy records.

7. **Accountability and scrutiny limits**
- Independent scrutiny of conflict claims was limited by PRC media controls and official opacity.
- Best evidence: U.S. State Department human-rights reporting, plus the structure of PRC information controls.

**Evidence Records**

1. **PRC peace language on Ukraine**
- **Claim:** In April 2023 Xi publicly reaffirmed political settlement language for Ukraine and repeated that nuclear weapons must not be used and nuclear wars must not be fought.
- **Why it matters:** It is the clearest official 2023 statement of Xi’s stated approach to a major international conflict.
- **Best source:** *Xi Jinping: China Ready to Issue a Joint Call with France for Political Settlement of the Ukraine Crisis*, PRC Ministry of Foreign Affairs, April 6, 2023. https://www.fmprc.gov.cn/eng/wjb/zzjg_663340/xos_664404/xwlb_664406/202304/t20230406_11055500.html
- **Locator:** Joint press-statement section on nuclear weapons, nuclear war, and political settlement.
- **Period fit:** Target-period.
- **Attribution basis:** Direct official statement by Xi in a diplomatic readout.
- **Complicating evidence:** This was rhetorical; it did not by itself show policy restraint in other security theaters.
- **Caution:** Official diplomatic messaging is curated.
- **Independent corroboration:** China’s broader UN and MFA peace statements in 2023.
- **Lens support:** `2B.3`, `2B.7`, `2B.10`; also overlaps with `2B.1` as rhetoric.

2. **China’s role in Saudi-Iran normalization**
- **Claim:** China helped broker the March 2023 Saudi-Iran rapprochement, which was a real diplomatic de-escalation in the Middle East.
- **Why it matters:** This is the main 2023 positive peace outcome attributable to Xi’s diplomacy.
- **Best source:** PRC official joint statement on the Saudi-Iran agreement, March 11, 2023. https://www.fmprc.gov.cn/eng/wjb/zzjg_663340/xybfs_663590/xwlb_663592/202303/t20230311_11039241.html
- **Locator:** Joint-statement text on resumption of diplomatic relations.
- **Period fit:** Target-period.
- **Attribution basis:** China’s direct diplomatic role as host and guarantor.
- **Complicating evidence:** The deal was also driven by Saudi and Iranian interests, not only Chinese initiative.
- **Caution:** PRC framing emphasizes Chinese success.
- **Independent corroboration:** Broad international reporting on the normalization deal.
- **Lens support:** `2B.1`, `2B.7`, `2B.10`, partially `2B.3`.

3. **Peaceful settlement framing on the Ukraine crisis**
- **Claim:** China’s February 24, 2023 position paper on Ukraine called for respect for sovereignty, cessation of hostilities, resumption of talks, and opposition to nuclear threats.
- **Why it matters:** It is the core formal peace claim of Xi’s government in the Ukraine context.
- **Best source:** *China’s Position on the Political Settlement of the Ukraine Crisis*, PRC MFA, February 24, 2023. https://www.fmprc.gov.cn/eng/wjb/zzjg_663340/dozys_664276/xwlb_664278/202302/t20230224_11030713.html
- **Locator:** Policy-point sections on ceasefire, dialogue, and nuclear safety.
- **Period fit:** Target-period.
- **Attribution basis:** Official policy document.
- **Complicating evidence:** The document avoided criticizing Russia in a way that would materially change Russia’s conduct.
- **Caution:** Broad principles, limited enforcement.
- **Independent corroboration:** Xi-Macron readout and later official diplomacy.
- **Lens support:** `2B.1`, `2B.3`, `2B.7`, `2B.10`.

4. **Xi’s phone call with Zelenskyy**
- **Claim:** Xi spoke with Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy by phone in April 2023, signaling a willingness to maintain contact with both sides of the war.
- **Why it matters:** This was a concrete diplomatic action, not just rhetoric.
- **Best source:** PRC MFA readout, April 26, 2023. https://www.fmprc.gov.cn/eng/gjhdq_665435/3265_665445/3250_664382/3252_664386/202304/t20230426_11066785.html
- **Locator:** Readout text on the call.
- **Period fit:** Target-period.
- **Attribution basis:** Direct presidential diplomacy.
- **Complicating evidence:** The call did not produce a peace process or changed Russian behavior.
- **Caution:** Readout is one-sided and omits any tension in the call.
- **Independent corroboration:** Reuters coverage of the call and its diplomatic significance.
- **Lens support:** `2B.1`, `2B.7`, `2B.10`.

5. **Taiwan coercive pressure**
- **Claim:** China conducted major military drills around Taiwan in April 2023, including simulated precision strikes.
- **Why it matters:** This is the strongest 2023 example of coercive security behavior that increased regional war risk.
- **Best source:** Reuters/AP reporting on April 8-9, 2023 Taiwan drills. https://apnews.com/article/2023-china-taiwan-drills-reuters
- **Locator:** Article on the drills and missile-force monitoring.
- **Period fit:** Target-period.
- **Attribution basis:** PLA activity under Xi’s military authority.
- **Complicating evidence:** Beijing framed the drills as deterrence and sovereignty defense.
- **Caution:** Operational details come from outside monitoring and reporting.
- **Independent corroboration:** Multiple Reuters reports and Taiwanese official statements.
- **Lens support:** `2B.2`, `2B.3`, `2B.8`, `2B.10`.

6. **South China Sea escalation**
- **Claim:** In December 2023 Chinese and Philippine vessels collided near Second Thomas Shoal amid water-cannon use and competing blame.
- **Why it matters:** This is a clear example of continued maritime coercion under Xi.
- **Best source:** Reuters report on the collision, December 2023. https://www.investing.com/news/world-news/philippines-china-trade-accusations-over-south-china-sea-collision-3253180
- **Locator:** Passages describing the collision, water cannon, and accusations.
- **Period fit:** Target-period.
- **Attribution basis:** PRC maritime enforcement and strategic policy.
- **Complicating evidence:** The Philippines also pursued its own assertive legal and operational posture.
- **Caution:** Each side offered different factual accounts.
- **Independent corroboration:** Related Reuters/AP reporting on South China Sea incidents through 2023.
- **Lens support:** `2B.2`, `2B.3`, `2B.5`, `2B.8`, `2B.10`.

7. **No major interstate war initiation**
- **Claim:** China did not initiate a new conventional interstate war in 2023, but it did sustain coercive and militarized pressure in key disputes.
- **Why it matters:** This is the basic conflict-exposure finding for the chapter.
- **Best sources:** Reuters/AP on Taiwan and South China Sea incidents; official diplomatic statements.
- **Locator:** The combined record of drills, maritime clashes, and diplomacy.
- **Period fit:** Target-period.
- **Attribution basis:** Overall pattern of state conduct.
- **Complicating evidence:** The absence of full-scale war does not mean restraint; coercion was persistent.
- **Caution:** Negative claim requires aggregation rather than one document.
- **Independent corroboration:** UCDP-style conflict datasets and contemporaneous reporting.
- **Lens support:** `2B.2`, `2B.10`.

8. **Lawful-security posture was selective**
- **Claim:** China maintained a peace-and-stability diplomatic line, but it did not join stronger nuclear disarmament constraints and continued to defend its own strategic latitude.
- **Why it matters:** This shows the gap between peaceful rhetoric and a fully law-bound security posture.
- **Best source:** UN Treaty Collection TPNW status page and PRC diplomatic statements. https://treaties.un.org/Pages/ViewDetails.aspx?chapter=26&clang=_en&mtdsg_no=XXVI-9&src=IND
- **Locator:** Treaty status lines showing China is not a party.
- **Period fit:** Target-period and current status.
- **Attribution basis:** Institutional treaty choice.
- **Complicating evidence:** China continued to participate in multilateral risk-reduction diplomacy.
- **Caution:** Treaty absence is only one measure of restraint.
- **Independent corroboration:** Arms Control Association analysis of NPT meetings.
- **Lens support:** `2B.7`, `2B.10`, partly `2B.3`.

9. **Limited independent scrutiny**
- **Claim:** Independent scrutiny of China’s conflict claims and security decisions remained constrained by censorship, surveillance, and political control of media and institutions.
- **Why it matters:** It affects confidence in any official account of peace, restraint, or lawful conduct.
- **Best source:** U.S. State Department 2023 human-rights report on China. https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107650.html
- **Locator:** Executive summary and sections on expression, censorship, detention, and judicial independence.
- **Period fit:** Target-period.
- **Attribution basis:** Information-environment analysis.
- **Complicating evidence:** Some foreign and Hong Kong outlets still reported on incidents, but access was incomplete.
- **Caution:** Human-rights reporting is broad and not conflict-specific.
- **Independent corroboration:** HRW and UN reporting on China’s information controls.
- **Lens support:** `2B.6`, `2B.9`, `2B.10`.

10. **China’s peace process diplomacy was real but not settlement-producing**
- **Claim:** In 2023 China acted as a diplomatic broker in some conflicts, but there was no durable China-led settlement architecture for the major disputes most relevant to this chapter.
- **Why it matters:** This is the core 2B.10 end-state judgment.
- **Best sources:** Saudi-Iran joint statement, Ukraine position paper, Xi-Zelenskyy call, South China Sea/Taiwan reporting.
- **Locator:** Cross-source pattern.
- **Period fit:** Target-period.
- **Attribution basis:** Aggregate record.
- **Complicating evidence:** The Saudi-Iran normalization was durable, but broader regional peace was not transformed.
- **Caution:** This is a synthesis, not a single source claim.
- **Independent corroboration:** Reuters, PRC MFA, and UN materials.
- **Lens support:** `2B.10`, with subsidiary support for `2B.1` and `2B.7`.

**Dispositions by Lens**
- `2B.1` Peaceful alternatives: **partially supported** by Ukraine diplomacy and Saudi-Iran mediation.
- `2B.10` Lasting international peace: **mixed**; one major de-escalation success, but overall coercive posture remained.
- `2B.2` Starting or prolonging war: **no evidence of China initiating a conventional interstate war**, but strong evidence of coercive pressure and escalation in Taiwan and the South China Sea.
- `2B.3` Honest justification for force: **mixed to adverse**; peace rhetoric was real, but often paired with strategic signaling and selective omission.
- `2B.4` Civilian and prisoner protection: **not materially engaged by the 2023 record here**; no direct conflict theater with prisoners comparable to an active war zone.
- `2B.5` Proxy and arms-recipient control: **weakly engaged**; no major proxy-control record surfaced in the inspected set.
- `2B.6` Scrutiny of war claims: **adverse/limited** because independent scrutiny was constrained.
- `2B.7` Ceasefires and settlements: **mixed**; meaningful Saudi-Iran de-escalation, but no broad settlement architecture.
- `2B.8` Responsible military resources: **adverse in part** because military resources supported coercive drills and maritime pressure.
- `2B.9` Accountability and remedy: **weak/limited**; no strong evidence of disclosure or remedy for coercive incidents.
  
**Source Groups**

**Accepted evidence**
- PRC MFA April 6, 2023 Xi-Macron Ukraine statement
- PRC MFA February 24, 2023 Ukraine position paper
- PRC MFA April 26, 2023 Xi-Zelenskyy call readout
- PRC official Saudi-Iran joint statement, March 11, 2023
- Reuters/AP April 2023 Taiwan drills
- Reuters December 2023 South China Sea collision
- U.S. State Department 2023 China report
- UN Treaty Collection TPNW status page

**Corroborating sources**
- Reuters and AP coverage of Taiwan and South China Sea incidents
- Arms Control Association reporting on 2023 NPT and P5 meetings
- UN and PRC diplomacy on Ukraine
- HRW and UN materials on information controls

**Uninspected leads**
- Reuters report: Xi told Biden Taiwan is the biggest, most dangerous issue in bilateral ties
- PRC MFA December 26, 2023 spokesperson briefing
- PRC official bilateral statement with the Philippines in January 2023
- Columbia book *War and Peace in the Taiwan Strait*
- UN / UCDP conflict summaries for 2023 China-related coercive incidents

**Source Counts**
- Developed evidence records: 9
- Context/corroboration sources: 5
- Uninspected leads remaining: 5+
- Clear gap: no strong evidence of Chinese involvement in a conventional war of choice in 2023; the record is instead one of coercive diplomacy, selective peace brokerage, and legal-rhetorical restraint.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 1B ---

**Orientation**
Xi Jinping’s 2023 international-security record combined selective peace diplomacy with coercive regional behavior and limited legal restraint. China brokered a real Saudi-Iran de-escalation, issued formal peace-language on Ukraine, and kept participating in multilateral arms-control talks, but it also sustained heavy pressure around Taiwan and the South China Sea and did not sign onto the strongest disarmament treaty. Information quality was uneven: official Chinese sources are strongest for formal positions and diplomatic acts, while outside reporting and specialist analysis are needed to judge actual restraint, escalation, and compliance.

**Compact Evidence Index**
1. **Ukraine peace diplomacy**
- China issued a formal Ukraine settlement paper and Xi later spoke directly with Zelenskyy.
- This is the main evidence for peaceful alternatives and settlement activity.

2. **Saudi-Iran normalization**
- China helped broker the March 2023 Saudi-Iran rapprochement.
- This is the clearest positive de-escalation outcome attributable to Xi’s diplomacy.

3. **Taiwan coercion**
- China conducted major drills around Taiwan in April 2023, including simulated strikes.
- This is strong evidence of coercive security behavior.

4. **South China Sea pressure**
- China and the Philippines continued to clash at sea in late 2023.
- This supports findings on coercive maritime conduct and prolonged tension.

5. **Arms-control posture**
- China stayed active in P5/NPT discussions and submitted UN working papers on nuclear risk reduction.
- But it remained outside the TPNW and did not bind itself to stronger disarmament constraints.

6. **Independent scrutiny limits**
- PRC censorship and institutional opacity constrained public checking of war claims and security decisions.
- This matters for accountability, honesty, and durable peace outcomes.

**Lens Disposition**
- `2B.1` Peaceful alternatives: **supported**
- `2B.10` Lasting international peace: **mixed**
- `2B.2` Starting or prolonging war: **no evidence of a China-initiated conventional war; strong evidence of coercive pressure**
- `2B.3` Honest justification for force: **mixed/adverse**
- `2B.4` Civilian and prisoner protection: **no material 2023 conflict-theater evidence found**
- `2B.5` Control of proxies and arms: **weakly supported / mostly no direct record**
- `2B.6` Scrutiny of war claims: **adverse**
- `2B.7` Ceasefires and settlements: **supported, but limited**
- `2B.8` Responsible military resources: **adverse in part**
- `2B.9` Accountability and remedy: **weak/limited**

**Accepted Evidence**
- [`WEB-2B-001`](https://www.fmprc.gov.cn/eng/wjb/zzjg_663340/xos_664404/xwlb_664406/202304/t20230406_11055500.html): Xi’s April 2023 Ukraine peace statement
- [`WEB-2B-002`](https://www.fmprc.gov.cn/eng/wjb/zzjg_663340/dozys_664276/xwlb_664278/202302/t20230224_11030713.html): China’s Ukraine position paper

- Reuters/AP reporting on Taiwan and South China Sea incidents
- PRC MFA and Xinhua diplomatic readouts
- Arms Control Association coverage of NPT/P5 diplomacy
- U.S. State Department reporting on PRC censorship, detention, and institutional opacity

**Uninspected Leads**
- Reuters report on Xi telling Biden Taiwan was the biggest and most dangerous issue
- PRC MFA January 2023 and June 2024 Philippines-related bilateral statements
- Columbia’s *War and Peace in the Taiwan Strait*
- Full UCDP/PA-X-style conflict summaries for 2023 China-related coercive incidents

**Source Counts**
- Developed evidence records: 9
- Corroborating/context sources: 4
- Uninspected leads: 4+
- Main gap: no evidence of a China-initiated conventional war in 2023; the record is coercion, diplomacy, and selective restraint rather than open war.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 2B ---

**Orientation**
Xi Jinping’s 2023 security record in the international-peace and lawful-security lane is mixed. China did not enter a new conventional war, but it did sustain coercive pressure around Taiwan and the South China Sea while also pursuing visible diplomacy on Ukraine and the Saudi-Iran rapprochement. The strongest pro-peace evidence is real but limited: China brokered the Saudi-Iran normalization deal, kept diplomatic contact with Ukraine, and continued formal language on political settlement and nuclear restraint. The main restraint on this chapter’s scoreless assessment is that Chinese peace rhetoric was not matched by broad legal commitment, independent scrutiny, or a materially more lawful security posture.

**Compact evidence index**
1. **Peaceful alternatives used**
- China did use diplomacy in 2023: Ukraine settlement paper, Xi-Zelenskyy call, and Saudi-Iran mediation.
- This supports peaceful-alternative and ceasefire/settlement lenses, but only partly.

2. **No China-initiated conventional war**
- The record shows coercion and militarized pressure, not a new Chinese war of choice.
- Taiwan drills and South China Sea incidents are the main adverse facts.

3. **Honest justification for force: mixed**
- PRC official language emphasized peace and stability, but the practical record shows selective restraint and coercive signaling.
- That creates a gap between rhetoric and conduct.

4. **Lawful security: selective, not strong**
- China stayed outside the TPNW and did not bind itself to stronger disarmament constraints.
- It participated in P5/NPT discussions and submitted risk-reduction papers, but those were not enough to show a durable lawful-security shift.

5. **Accountability and scrutiny were limited**
- PRC information controls and institutional opacity constrained verification of conflict claims and security decisions.
- That weakens confidence in any claim of responsible conduct.

**Evidence records**
1. **Ukraine peace statement**
- **Claim:** Xi publicly reaffirmed political settlement language on Ukraine in April 2023 and repeated that nuclear weapons must not be used and nuclear wars must not be fought.
- **Why it matters:** This is the clearest formal peace statement tied directly to Xi in 2023.
- **Source:** PRC Ministry of Foreign Affairs, *Xi Jinping: China Ready to Issue a Joint Call with France for Political Settlement of the Ukraine Crisis*, April 6, 2023. https://www.fmprc.gov.cn/eng/wjb/zzjg_663340/xos_664404/xwlb_664406/202304/t20230406_11055500.html
- **Locator:** Joint press statement section on nuclear weapons, nuclear war, and political settlement.
- **Period:** Target-period.
- **Attribution:** Direct statement by Xi.
- **Cautions:** Diplomatic rhetoric; no enforcement mechanism.
- **Corroboration:** PRC Ukraine position paper; Xi-Zelenskyy call readout.
- **Lens support:** `2B.1`, `2B.3`, `2B.7`, `2B.10`.

2. **Ukraine policy paper**
- **Claim:** China issued a formal position paper calling for sovereignty respect, cessation of hostilities, resumption of talks, and opposition to nuclear threats in the Ukraine war.
- **Why it matters:** This is the core formal policy statement for China’s peace posture in the Ukraine conflict.
- **Source:** PRC MFA, *China’s Position on the Political Settlement of the Ukraine Crisis*, February 24, 2023. https://www.fmprc.gov.cn/eng/wjb/zzjg_663340/dozys_664276/xwlb_664278/202302/t20230224_11030713.html
- **Locator:** Policy-point sections on ceasefire, dialogue, and nuclear safety.
- **Period:** Target-period.
- **Attribution:** Institutional Chinese policy.
- **Cautions:** Avoids directly confronting Russia; not a binding settlement framework.
- **Corroboration:** Xi-Macron statement; Xi-Zelenskyy call.
- **Lens support:** `2B.1`, `2B.3`, `2B.7`, `2B.10`.

3. **Xi-Zelenskyy call**
- **Claim:** Xi held a direct phone call with Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy in April 2023.
- **Why it matters:** Concrete diplomatic engagement with both sides of the war.
- **Source:** PRC MFA, *President Xi Jinping Speaks with Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy on the Phone*, April 26, 2023. https://www.fmprc.gov.cn/eng/gjhdq_665435/3265_665445/3250_664382/3252_664386/202304/t20230426_11066785.html
- **Locator:** Official call readout.
- **Period:** Target-period.
- **Attribution:** Direct diplomacy.
- **Cautions:** No visible peace-process breakthrough followed.
- **Corroboration:** Reuters coverage of the call.
- **Lens support:** `2B.1`, `2B.7`, `2B.10`.

4. **Saudi-Iran normalization**
- **Claim:** China helped broker the March 2023 Saudi-Iran normalization agreement, producing a real diplomatic de-escalation.
- **Why it matters:** This is the strongest positive peace outcome in the 2023 record.
- **Source:** PRC MFA joint statement, March 11, 2023. https://www.fmprc.gov.cn/eng/wjb/zzjg_663340/xybfs_663590/xwlb_663592/202303/t20230311_11039241.html
- **Locator:** Joint-statement text on resumption of diplomatic relations.
- **Period:** Target-period.
- **Attribution:** Direct Chinese brokerage.
- **Cautions:** The deal also reflected Saudi and Iranian interests.
- **Corroboration:** Broad international reporting on the agreement.
- **Lens support:** `2B.1`, `2B.7`, `2B.10`, `2B.3`.

5. **Taiwan drills**
- **Claim:** China conducted major military drills around Taiwan in April 2023, including simulated precision strikes.
- **Why it matters:** This is strong evidence of coercive security behavior and elevated conflict risk.
- **Source:** AP/Reuters, *China conducts large military drills around Taiwan*, April 9, 2023. https://apnews.com/article/2023-china-taiwan-drills-reuters
- **Locator:** Passages on drills and missile-force monitoring.
- **Period:** Target-period.
- **Attribution:** PLA action under Xi’s military authority.
- **Cautions:** Beijing described the activity as deterrence and sovereignty defense.
- **Corroboration:** Reuters/Taiwan reporting on the same drills.
- **Lens support:** `2B.2`, `2B.3`, `2B.8`, `2B.10`.

6. **South China Sea collision**
- **Claim:** Chinese and Philippine vessels collided near Second Thomas Shoal in December 2023 amid water-cannon use and competing blame.
- **Why it matters:** This shows sustained maritime coercion rather than peaceful stabilization.
- **Source:** Reuters, *Philippines, China trade accusations over South China Sea collision*, December 10, 2023. https://www.investing.com/news/world-news/philippines-china-trade-accusations-over-south-china-sea-collision-3253180
- **Locator:** Passages describing the collision and accusations.
- **Period:** Target-period.
- **Attribution:** PRC maritime policy and operations.
- **Cautions:** Competing factual accounts from the two sides.
- **Corroboration:** Reuters/AP coverage of related 2023 incidents.
- **Lens support:** `2B.2`, `2B.3`, `2B.5`, `2B.8`, `2B.10`.

7. **TPNW non-adherence**
- **Claim:** China remained outside the Treaty on the Prohibition of Nuclear Weapons.
- **Why it matters:** This is the clearest formal marker that China did not join the strongest disarmament regime.
- **Source:** UN Treaty Collection, TPNW status page. https://treaties.un.org/Pages/ViewDetails.aspx?chapter=26&clang=_en&mtdsg_no=XXVI-9&src=IND
- **Locator:** Status lines showing parties/signatories and no Chinese accession.
- **Period:** Target-period and current status.
- **Attribution:** Institutional treaty choice.
- **Cautions:** Treaty absence alone does not prove hostility to restraint.
- **Corroboration:** PRC peace rhetoric and NPT participation.
- **Lens support:** `2B.7`, `2B.10`, `2B.3`.

8. **P5/NPT engagement**
- **Claim:** China participated in P5/NPT discussions in 2023 about reducing the risk of nuclear war.
- **Why it matters:** This supports the view that China was not isolating itself from arms-control diplomacy.
- **Source:** Arms Control Association, *NPT Nuclear-Weapon States Meet in Dubai*, March 2023. https://www.armscontrol.org/act/2023-03/news-briefs/npt-nuclear-weapon-states-meet-dubai
- **Locator:** Lines 79-82.
- **Period:** Target-period.
- **Attribution:** Direct participation.
- **Cautions:** Dialogue participation does not establish substantive restraint.
- **Corroboration:** China’s UN nuclear-risk working papers.
- **Lens support:** `2B.1`, `2B.7`, `2B.10`.

9. **Limits on scrutiny**
- **Claim:** The U.S. State Department’s 2023 China report described arbitrary detention, torture, lack of judicial independence, censorship, surveillance, and severe restrictions on political participation.
- **Why it matters:** This is central to evaluating whether China allowed independent scrutiny of conflict claims and security decisions.
- **Source:** U.S. State Department 2023 China report, via ECOI. https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107650.html
- **Locator:** Executive summary and sections on expression, detention, and judicial independence.
- **Period:** Target-period.
- **Attribution:** Information-environment assessment.
- **Cautions:** Broad report, not conflict-specific.
- **Corroboration:** HRW/UN reporting on similar controls.
- **Lens support:** `2B.6`, `2B.9`, `2B.10`.

**Disposition by lens**
- `2B.1` Peaceful alternatives: **supported**
- `2B.10` Lasting international peace: **mixed**
- `2B.2` Starting or prolonging war: **no China-initiated conventional war found; coercive pressure evident**
- `2B.3` Honest justification for force: **mixed**
- `2B.4` Civilian and prisoner protection: **no direct conflict-theater record found**
- `2B.5` Control of proxies and arms: **weak / no direct record**
- `2B.6` Scrutiny of war claims: **adverse**
- `2B.7` Ceasefires and settlements: **supported, but limited**
- `2B.8` Responsible military resources: **adverse in part**
- `2B.9` Accountability and remedy: **weak**

**Source groups**
**Accepted evidence**
- PRC MFA Ukraine position paper
- PRC MFA Xi-Macron Ukraine statement
- PRC MFA Xi-Zelenskyy call readout
- PRC MFA Saudi-Iran joint statement
- AP/Reuters Taiwan drills report
- Reuters South China Sea collision report
- UN Treaty Collection TPNW status page
- Arms Control Association P5/Dubai brief
- U.S. State Department 2023 China report

**Corroboration**
- Reuters/AP coverage of Taiwan and South China Sea incidents
- Arms Control Association NPT/P5 reporting
- PRC official diplomacy on Ukraine
- Human-rights reporting on censorship and detention

**Uninspected leads**
- Reuters report on Xi telling Biden Taiwan was the biggest and most dangerous issue
- PRC MFA Philippines-related bilateral statements
- Columbia *War and Peace in the Taiwan Strait*
- Additional conflict-dataset summaries or maritime incident records

**Research gaps**
- No evidence of a China-initiated conventional war in 2023.
- No strong evidence of civilian-harm or prisoner-protection issues in a China-foreign-war setting.
- No direct evidence of proxy-war control problems.
- The main unresolved issue is how much of China’s peace language translated into durable lawful restraint rather than tactical diplomacy.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 3B ---

**Orientation**
Xi Jinping’s 2023 domestic safety record is dominated by repression and control rather than protection. The state tightly controlled dissent, commemorations, religion, and online speech; used surveillance and detention as routine tools; and retained a system with weak independent oversight. There was no comparable record of independent civilian protection, accountable restraint, or meaningful remedy for abuse. The main contextual caveat is that many harms were concentrated in a broader authoritarian governance system, but Xi’s top-party and CMC authority makes the national policy direction attributable to him in substantial part.

**Compact evidence index**
1. **State abuse and arbitrary detention**
- The strongest 2023 evidence shows continuing arbitrary detention, torture risks, and lack of judicial independence.
- Best sources: U.S. State Department 2023 China report; HRW UPR submission; CAT/CESCR/UN records.

2. **Protest and dissent repression**
- China tightened Tiananmen access and Hong Kong detentions continued around June 4.
- Best sources: AP coverage; Freedom House Dissent Monitor; human-rights reporting.

3. **Coercive surveillance and online control**
- CAC “Qinglang” campaigns targeted online disorder, youth content, and “media chaos,” showing broad administrative control.
- Best sources: CAC notices; censorship-related NGO reporting.

4. **Oversight and remedy remained weak**
- Independent complaint and remedy channels were not meaningfully open.
- Best sources: State Department report; OHCHR communications; HRC/CAT materials.

5. **Protection of vulnerable groups was partial and uneven**
- There were official claims of protection, but they do not outweigh the broader pattern of repression and selective enforcement.
- Best sources: PRC white papers and legal notices, corroborated by outside reporting.

6. **Safety without state terror: negative overall**
- The country was not experiencing large-scale communal war or insurgency, but the state itself was a primary source of fear and coercion.
- Best sources: Freedom House, Amnesty, HRW, AP.

**Evidence records**
1. **Arbitrary detention and torture risk**
- **Claim:** In 2023 China continued to use arbitrary detention, torture or cruel treatment, and politically driven imprisonment, with no effective judicial independence.
- **Why it matters:** This is the core 3B adverse fact: the state did not protect people from abuse and remained a primary source of it.
- **Source:** U.S. Department of State, *2023 Country Reports on Human Rights Practices: China*, April 22, 2024. https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107650.html
- **Locator:** Executive summary and sections on arbitrary detention, torture, and judicial independence.
- **Period:** Target-period.
- **Attribution:** Institutional national policy and enforcement under Xi.
- **Cautions:** Broad annual report; not every abuse is individually documented.
- **Corroboration:** HRW UPR submission; CAT observations; Amnesty reporting.
- **Lens support:** `3B.1`, `3B.2`, `3B.4`, `3B.10`.

2. **Repression deepened under Xi’s third term**
- **Claim:** Human Rights Watch said repression deepened under Xi’s third term and that authorities tightened control over civil society, media, the internet, and surveillance in 2023.
- **Why it matters:** This directly addresses ruler responsibility for an environment of fear and weak protection.
- **Source:** HRW, *Submission to Universal Periodic Review: China*, July 27, 2023. https://www.hrw.org/news/2023/07/27/submission-universal-periodic-review-china
- **Locator:** Lines 585-598.
- **Period:** Target-period.
- **Attribution:** Direct state policy under Xi.
- **Cautions:** Advocacy source, but detailed and specific.
- **Corroboration:** State Department report; Freedom House.
- **Lens support:** `3B.1`, `3B.2`, `3B.4`, `3B.5`, `3B.10`.

3. **June 4 detention and Tiananmen access restrictions**
- **Claim:** China tightened access to Tiananmen Square and Hong Kong police detained people around the June 4 anniversary in 2023.
- **Why it matters:** This is concrete evidence of repression of protest memory and public assembly.
- **Source:** AP, *China tightens access to Tiananmen Square, 20 detained in Hong Kong on anniversary of 1989 protests*, June 4, 2023. https://abc17news.com/news/ap-national-news/2023/06/04/china-tightens-access-to-tiananmen-square-20-detained-in-hong-kong-on-anniversary-of-1989-protests
- **Locator:** Lede and body on detentions and commemorations.
- **Period:** Target-period.
- **Attribution:** National environment of repression, especially through Hong Kong security policy and mainland policing.
- **Cautions:** Hong Kong enforcement is not identical to mainland policing, though it remains part of the PRC security order.
- **Corroboration:** Freedom House Dissent Monitor; AP follow-up coverage.
- **Lens support:** `3B.3`, `3B.5`, `3B.8`, `3B.10`.

4. **Freedom House dissent environment**
- **Claim:** Freedom House documented an ongoing pattern of protest suppression and political risk around dissent in early 2023.
- **Why it matters:** It provides a contemporaneous independent monitor of the repression environment.
- **Source:** Freedom House, *China Dissent Monitor: Issue 3 (January-March 2023)*. https://freedomhouse.org/report/china-dissent-monitor/2023/issue-3-january-march-2023
- **Locator:** Issue summary and incident notes.
- **Period:** Target-period.
- **Attribution:** National control system under Xi.
- **Cautions:** Monitor focuses on recorded incidents, not a complete census.
- **Corroboration:** AP, HRW, State Department.
- **Lens support:** `3B.3`, `3B.6`, `3B.8`, `3B.10`.

5. **Censorship and surveillance campaigns**
- **Claim:** China’s Cyberspace Administration continued 2023 “Qinglang” cleanup campaigns targeting online disorder, minors, and so-called self-media chaos.
- **Why it matters:** This shows active administrative control over speech and information, contributing to a climate of fear and weak oversight.
- **Source:** Cyberspace Administration of China notices, January 18, March 12, June 27, and November 17, 2023. Example: https://www.cac.gov.cn/2023-01/18/c_1675676650690321.htm
- **Locator:** Project notices announcing the campaigns.
- **Period:** Target-period.
- **Attribution:** Central administrative policy under Xi.
- **Cautions:** Official notices frame these as order-maintenance; outside observers treat them as censorship tools.
- **Corroboration:** State Department and HRW reports on censorship and surveillance.
- **Lens support:** `3B.3`, `3B.5`, `3B.8`, `3B.9`, `3B.10`.

6. **Weak independent oversight and remedy**
- **Claim:** China’s 2023 rights environment left little room for independent courts, complaint systems, or effective remedies for state abuse.
- **Why it matters:** A domestic safety chapter requires not just absence of violence but an ability to report and correct abuse.
- **Source:** U.S. State Department report via ECOI. https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107650.html
- **Locator:** Sections on judicial independence, detention, and retaliation.
- **Period:** Target-period.
- **Attribution:** National institutional structure under Xi.
- **Cautions:** Summary source, but well supported by multiple monitors.
- **Corroboration:** OHCHR communications; CAT observations.
- **Lens support:** `3B.4`, `3B.9`, `3B.10`.

7. **UN treaty-body concerns**
- **Claim:** UN treaty bodies in 2023 continued to raise concerns about torture, detention, due process, and the lack of effective remedies in China.
- **Why it matters:** Independent international review corroborates the abuse environment.
- **Sources:** OHCHR/CAT concluding observations and communications; UN records. Examples:
- https://docstore.ohchr.org/SelfServices/FilesHandler.ashx?enc=%2F3J9ik6RORM6KgPB5%2B23iIiVoWwQKj6gvAqc5fABj0G7zvvMq07oQj%2BYnBBIw4mbwx6VGmLPW%2Fc7m2Jcq4mqQw%3D%3D
- https://docstore.ohchr.org/SelfServices/FilesHandler.ashx?enc=HE07rID4oW1J%2FT6SGsLPA0zC%2B0gcmO12drmCejE3sZ6mQh3Ew9c8ZNqQIjiORVTsiA2DSrLs9szhooOyxeN8Tg%3D%3D
- **Locator:** Committee observations and related paragraphs on detention and torture.
- **Period:** Mostly inherited but still live in 2023.
- **Attribution:** Structural state practice.
- **Cautions:** Treaty-body language is rights-focused and may not reflect all positive local variation.
- **Corroboration:** State Department, HRW, Amnesty.
- **Lens support:** `3B.1`, `3B.4`, `3B.10`.

8. **Formal claims of protection in Tibet/Xizang**
- **Claim:** PRC white-paper material on Xizang asserted rights protection and improved social stability, but it is official justification rather than independent evidence of restraint.
- **Why it matters:** It is the main positive official claim and should be retained for contrast.
- **Source:** State Council Information Office, *Human Rights in Xizang in the New Era*. https://english.scio.gov.cn/node_9015313.html
- **Locator:** White-paper overview and claims sections.
- **Period:** Target-period and retrospective framing.
- **Attribution:** Official policy narrative.
- **Cautions:** Strong propaganda value; needs independent corroboration.
- **Corroboration:** Very limited; mostly outside criticism.
- **Lens support:** `3B.7`, `3B.9`, context for `3B.10`.

9. **Exit bans and transnational coercion**
- **Claim:** Safeguard Defenders documented continued and expanding use of exit bans in China, affecting vulnerable people and critics.
- **Why it matters:** Exit bans are a domestic safety issue because they trap people and can function as coercive punishment without due process.
- **Source:** Safeguard Defenders, *Trapped: China’s Expanding Use of Exit Bans*. https://safeguarddefenders.com/en/publications/trapped-chinas-expanding-use-exit-bans
- **Locator:** Report sections on legal basis and practice.
- **Period:** Target-period and broader Xi period.
- **Attribution:** National coercive apparatus.
- **Cautions:** NGO source; practice is hard to quantify precisely.
- **Corroboration:** State Department and UN records on arbitrary restrictions.
- **Lens support:** `3B.1`, `3B.2`, `3B.4`, `3B.10`.

10. **No evidence of reduced violence without fear**
- **Claim:** China did not show a meaningful 2023 shift toward safety without state terror; lower open violence coexisted with intensified coercive control.
- **Why it matters:** This is the chapter’s key negative synthesis.
- **Source basis:** Aggregate of State Department, HRW, Freedom House, CAC, AP.
- **Locator:** Cross-source pattern.
- **Period:** Target-period.
- **Attribution:** National governance under Xi.
- **Cautions:** This is a synthesis, not a single-source fact.
- **Corroboration:** All above.
- **Lens support:** `3B.6`, `3B.10`.

**Disposition by lens**
- `3B.1` Protection from state abuse: **adverse**
- `3B.10` Lasting domestic safety: **adverse**
- `3B.2` Control of coercive forces: **adverse**
- `3B.3` Incitement and targeted hatred: **adverse/mixed**
- `3B.4` Complaints and independent oversight: **adverse**
- `3B.5` Emergency and surveillance powers: **adverse**
- `3B.6` Safety without state terror: **adverse**
- `3B.7` Protection of vulnerable groups: **mixed but weak**
- `3B.8` Safe protest and dissent: **adverse**
- `3B.9` Restrained crisis response: **adverse/mixed**

**Source groups**

**Accepted evidence**
- U.S. State Department 2023 China report
- HRW UPR submission
- AP Tiananmen/Hong Kong report
- Freedom House Dissent Monitor
- CAC Qinglang notices
- OHCHR/CAT records
- Safeguard Defenders exit-bans report
- SCIO Xizang white paper

**Corroboration**
- Amnesty International annual reporting
- OHCHR special-procedure communications
- AP and other news coverage of protest repression
- Freedom House and NGO monitoring of censorship and dissent

**Uninspected leads**
- BBC Yunnan mosque-clash report
- OHCHR special-procedure response records beyond the ones already opened
- The AP feature on protest flare-ups
- Additional local-language civil-society or legal materials on detention and remedy

**Research gaps**
- I did not find a strong 2023 record of independent, effective remedies for abuse.
- There is no evidence of a national shift toward rights-protective policing.
- The main unresolved issue is not whether abuse existed, but how much of the observed repression was centrally directed versus locally executed within the broader Xi-era system.
